OIG Report of Veterans Health Administration’s
``
Responses on Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2026
Executive Summary
The Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act of 2014 and VA Choice and Quality
Employment Act of 2017 mandates the VA Office of Inspector General (OIG) to identify,
annually, a minimum of five clinical and five nonclinical Veterans Health Administration (VHA)
occupations with the largest staffing shortages within each VHA medical center (facility).1
Pursuant to this requirement, the OIG distributed a questionnaire to VHA officials and collected
their opinions of severe staffing shortages by occupation. The OIG does not validate the
accuracy of the information provided by the VHA in response to the questionnaire. As a result,
this report is a descriptive review of VHA reporting and is intended for informational purposes
based on a specific point in time.
A severe shortage refers to occupations that are difficult to fill based on the criteria in 5 C.F.R.
§ 337.204, Severe Shortage of Candidates. This designation does not necessarily indicate the
presence of vacancies.2 A vacancy, by contrast, is an individual, unfilled position and is separate
from the severe‑shortage classification. For example, a facility may designate an occupation as
experiencing a severe shortage whether it has zero vacancies—or 100 vacancies. The severe-
shortage criteria, as outlined in 5 C.F.R. § 337.204, are applied in this report pursuant to statutory
requirements (see 38 U.S.C. § 7412), which direct the OIG to identify occupations with the
largest staffing shortages so that the VA Secretary may consider special hiring authorities for
those occupations.3 VHA’s annual staffing shortage report uses the same definition of severe
shortage.4
This is the 13th OIG annual report on occupational staffing shortages pursuant to the
Congressional mandate and the 9th to report severe occupational staffing shortages as identified
by staff for each facility. To maintain consistency with the prior reports, the OIG deployed a
questionnaire to VHA-identified officials to respond on behalf of each facility. These VHA
officials completed the questionnaire by selecting occupations considered to have severe staffing
shortages by selecting from a list of occupations, using the criteria from C.F.R. § 337.204. The
results reflect the selections of these VHA officials based on the perception of staffing shortages
at the facility level, not an independent assessment by the OIG.
Key findings from this year’s report include:

OIG Report of Veterans Health Administration’s Responses on Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2026
· In fiscal year (FY) 2026, which runs from October 1, 2025, through
September 30, 2026, VHA officials reported a total of 4,712 severe occupational
staffing shortages.
· VHA officials identified severe shortages in 389 occupations.
· In FY 2026, 48 occupations were identified as severe staffing shortages for at least
20 percent of facilities by VHA officials.
· VHA officials reported that 97 percent of facilities had severe occupational staffing
shortages for Medical Officer occupations, and 86 percent of facilities had severe
shortages for Nurse occupations.
· Practical Nurse was the most frequently reported clinical severe occupational
staffing shortage in FY 2026, the most frequently reported Hybrid Title 38
occupation, and the most frequently reported severe shortage occupation overall,
with VHA officials reporting for 59 percent of facilities.
· Police was reported as a shortage by VHA officials for 57 percent of facilities,
making it the most frequently reported nonclinical shortage occupation.
VHA officials for 139 VHA facilities identified staffing shortages.
The OIG made no recommendations based on this report.
VA Comments and OIG Response
The Under Secretary for Health concurred with the report (seeappendix E)5. No further action
is required.

Responses on Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2026
Introduction
The purpose of this review is for the VA Office of Inspector General (OIG) to report a minimum
of five clinical and five nonclinical Veterans Health Administration (VHA) occupations that
have the largest staffing shortages at each VHA medical center (facility) as required by the
Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act (VACAA) of 2014 and VA Choice and
Quality Employment Act (VCQEA) of 2017.6 VACAA and VCQEA also established authority
for the VA Secretary to directly recruit and appoint qualified personnel to occupations as
identified by the OIG as having the largest staffing shortages.
The OIG deployed a questionnaire to VHA-selected officials to identify severe occupational
staffing shortages at each facility. VHA officials for each facility identified severe staffing
shortages by selecting from a list of occupations after applying the criteria from 5 C.F.R. §
337.204, Severe Shortage of Candidates.7 This is the 13th iteration in a series of annual OIG
reports determining occupational staffing shortages in VHA and the 9th to identify severe
occupational staffing shortages at the facility level.8 As in the past, the information in this report
is based on what is reported by VHA officials in response to a questionnaire, and the OIG has
not validated the accuracy of the information provided in response to the questionnaire. As a
result, this report is a descriptive review and is intended for informational purposes.

=== PAGE 8 ===
OIG Report of Veterans Health Administration’s Responses on Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2026
Background
In May 2014, the OIG reported ongoing concerns regarding access to VHA care, scheduling
practices, and excessive wait times at the VA Phoenix Health Care System.9 Congress passed
VACAA in response to these concerns. VACAA required the OIG to determine, annually, the
five VHA occupations with the largest staffing shortages. VCQEA expanded the scope of the
OIG’s work to include a minimum of five clinical and five nonclinical VHA occupations with
the largest reported staffing shortages at each facility.
Scope and Methodology
Staffing Questionnaire Development and Distribution
The OIG is required by 38 U.S.C. § 7412(a) to “determine, … at a minimum, the five clinical
and five nonclinical occupations” with severe shortages at each facility.10 The OIG distributed a
link to a web-based questionnaire via email to one VHA-identified official per facility beginning
on March 18, 2026, with a required completion date of April 1, 2026.11 The questionnaire asked
VHA officials to identify occupations with a severe shortage of candidates based on criteria from
5 C.F.R. § 337.204. The OIG confirmed receipt of completed questionnaires from 126 VHA
officials on behalf of all 139 facilities on April 16, 2026.
The OIG provided VHA officials with the following criteria from 5 C.F.R. § 337.204 for
determining a severe shortage of candidates:
(1)The results of workforce planning and analysis;
(2)Employment trends including the local or national labor market;
(3)The existence of nationwide or geographic skills shortages;
(4)Agency efforts, including recruitment initiatives, use of other appointing authorities
(e.g., schedule A, schedule B) and flexibilities, training and development programs
9 VA OIG,Interim Report: Review of Patient Wait Times, Scheduling Practices, and Alleged Patient Deaths at the
Phoenix Health Care System,Report No. 14-02603-178, May 28, 2014.
10 38 U.S.C § 7412(a). VA Choice and Quality Employment Act, § 201.VCQEA modified 38 U.S. Code § 7412
specifying that the OIG determine the five clinical and five nonclinical occupations with the largest staffing
shortages. VHA National Workforce Planning, Workforce Management and Consulting, Workforce Solutions,
FY 2024 VHA Workforce and Succession Strategic Plan for FY 2025-2028 defines clinical occupations to “include
personnel who provide direct patient care or services incident to patient care and whose efforts have a therapeutic
intent (e.g., physical, mental, social and spiritual).” VHA defines nonclinical occupations as “administrative and
maintenance personnel who do not provide direct patient care or services incident to patient care.”

tailored to the position(s), and an explanation of why these recruitment and training
efforts have not been sufficient;
(5)The availability and quality of candidates;
(6)The desirability of the geographic location of the position(s);
(7)The desirability of the duties and/or work environment associated with the position(s);
and
(8)Other pertinent information such as selective placement factors or other special
requirements of the position, as well as agency use of hiring flexibilities such as
recruitment or retention allowances or special salary rates.
The OIG did not assess whether VHA officials had documentation to support their determination
that an occupation met the severe-shortage criteria.
The severe‑shortage criteria can be used to inform the VA Secretary’s authority under 38 U.S.C.
§ 7412 to recruit and directly appoint certain occupations based on the OIG’s reporting of
occupational staffing shortages. VHA hires specific occupations under different statutory
authorities. VHA typically follows competitive hiring processes, including veterans’ preference
rules, when hiring for occupations appointed under Title 5. Under Title 38, VHA appoints
individuals to occupations such as Medical Officer and Nurse without regard to Title 5 civil
service requirements. VHA appoints Hybrid Title 38 occupations such as 0180 Psychology under
Title 38 but typically follows Title 5 veterans’ preference rules in hiring.
The OIG did not consider vacancy data when identifying occupations reported as severe staffing
shortages across VHA. Designation as a severe staffing shortage does not necessarily mean an
occupation has vacant positions. A vacancy is an authorized but unfilled position and is distinct
from a severe staffing shortage designation. For example, a facility could identify an occupation
as experiencing a severe staffing shortage regardless of whether it had no vacant positions or 100
vacant positions. The OIG uses the same definition of staffing shortages as VHA's annual
shortage occupation report.12
The Office of Personnel Management (OPM), after consulting with federal agencies, created
occupational series to classify positions with similar work specializations and qualifications; for
instance, the 0602 Medical Officer series. VHA delineates Medical Officer and Nurse specialties
using assignment codes within the corresponding OPM occupational series. For example, an
Infection Control Nurse would have an OPM occupational series of 0610 Nurse, and a VHA
assignment code of 74.
12 VHA Workforce Management and Consulting, 2025 VHA Shortage Occupation Report.
VA OIG 26-01046-271 | Page 3 | September 11, 2026

=== PAGE 10 ===
OIG Report of Veterans Health Administration’s Responses on Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2026
The OIG’s questionnaire listed OPM occupational series used by VHA, as well as VHA
assignment codes for Medical Officer and Nurse specialties.13Seeappendix Afor a complete list
of OPM occupational series and VHA assignment codes included in the questionnaire. VHA
officials were asked to indicate those occupations considered to have a severe occupational
staffing shortage and also had the option to report that a facility had no severe occupational
staffing shortages.
Questionnaire Categorization
The OIG reported an occupation as a severe occupational staffing shortage when it was
designated as such by the VHA official. The OIG counted the total number of times the
occupation was identified as a severe occupational staffing shortage among all responses to
determine shortages across VHA.
VHA officials could identify severe shortages for Medical Officer and Nurse occupations at the
Office of Personnel Management (OPM) occupational series level, the VHA assignment code
level, or both. To provide greater detail, the OIG reported individual Medical Officer and Nurse
assignment code occupations in its list of the most severe occupational staffing shortage.
Separately, for purposes of summarizing shortages by occupational series, the OIG considered
the Medical Officer or Nurse series to have a severe shortage if VHA officials identified either
the OPM occupational series or any related VHA assignment code as having a severe shortage.
The OIG is required to identify at a minimum five clinical and five nonclinical occupations with
severe occupational shortages.14 To better inform the VA Secretary’s ability to recruit and
directly appoint applicants into occupations with occupational shortages, the OIG identified
severe staffing shortages reported by VHA officials for at least 20 percent of facilities. Reporting
only occupations that were designated as a severe occupational staffing shortage for at least
20 percent of facilities is consistent with the criterion for a national shortage of occupations used
by VA Workforce Management and Consulting in its annual shortage occupation report.
Authorities
In the absence of current VA or VHA policy, the OIG considered previous guidance to be in
effect until superseded by an updated or recertified directive, handbook, or other policy
document on the same or similar issue(s).
Oversight authority to review the programs and operations of VA medical facilities is authorized
by the Inspector General Act of 1978, as amended, 5 U.S.C. §§ 401–424. The OIG reviews

Review Results
The OIG aggregated the number of severe occupational staffing shortages for all occupations
reported by VHA officials. The OIG also categorized the top five clinical and five nonclinical
shortages, as well as occupations reported as severe shortages by VHA officials for at least
20 percent of facilities (see appendix D). Finally, the OIG included a list of all reported
occupations by facility (see appendix B).
Total Number of Severe Occupational Shortages
Between March and April 2026, VHA officials for 139 facilities reported a total of 4,712 severe
occupational staffing shortages. Those 4,712 shortages were drawn from 389 occupations.
Because each VHA official can identify shortages in any occupation for each facility, and
multiple VHA officials often report shortages in the same occupation, the total number of
shortages reflects repeated selections of the same occupations across facilities.
The OIG also counted severe occupational shortages reported at the facility level. VHA officials
for all 139 facilities reported staffing shortages. The number of severe occupational staffing
shortage designations by VHA official ranged from 4 (VA Gulf Coast Healthcare System) to 148
(Birmingham VA Health Care System), including both clinical and nonclinical occupations.15
The greatest number of clinical occupational shortages reported by an individual VHA official
was 108 (Birmingham VA Health Care System), and the greatest number of nonclinical
occupational shortages was 41 (VA North Texas Health Care System).16 (See appendix C).
Medical Officer and Nurse Occupational Staffing Shortages
Because VHA uses assignment codes to designate specialties within the corresponding OPM
occupational series, Medical Officer and Nurse occupations were categorized separately from the
other occupations. Using the information gathered via the questionnaire, the OIG derived the
frequency of Medical Officer and Nurse severe occupational staffing shortages from VHA
officials indicating either the OPM occupational series or any of its related VHA assignment
codes as shortages. For FY 2026, Medical Officer was reported as a shortage for 97 percent of
15 VA Gulf Coast Healthcare System in Biloxi, Mississippi (VISN 16, Station 520) reported the fewest severe
occupational staffing shortages and Birmingham VA Health Care System in Alabama (VISN 7, Station 521)
reported the most severe occupational staffing shortages among all facilities.
16 Birmingham VA Health Care System in Alabama (VISN 7, Station 521) designated the most clinical severe

Top Occupational Shortages
In FY 2026, VHA officials identified 48 occupations as severe staffing shortages for at least
20 percent of facilities. (See appendix D for the complete list).
Top Five Clinical Shortage Occupations
Practical Nurse was the most frequently reported clinical severe occupational staffing shortage in
FY 2026, and the most frequent severe shortage occupation overall, with VHA officials reporting
it as a shortage for 59 percent of facilities (82 of 139).

In FY 2026, the OIG found that VHA officials for 57 percent of facilities (79 of 139) designated

Conclusion
In FY 2026, VHA officials reported a total of 4,712 severe occupational staffing shortages across
389 occupations. VHA officials identified at least one occupation as experiencing a severe
staffing shortage for every facility. Overall, 48 occupations were identified as severe staffing
shortages by VHA officials for at least 20 percent of facilities.
Ninety-seven percent of facilities were identified by VHA officials as having a severe staffing
shortage for the Medical Officer occupational series, and 86 percent were identified as having a
severe shortage for the Nurse occupational series. Among individual clinical occupations,
Practical Nurse was the most frequently reported severe staffing shortage, identified by VHA
officials for 59 percent of facilities. The remaining top five clinical occupations with severe
staffing shortages were Psychology, Psychiatry, Social Work, and Nursing Assistant.
VHA officials for 57 percent of facilities noted severe occupational staffing shortages for Police,
making it the most frequently reported nonclinical occupation. Other occupations reported in the
top five nonclinical occupations included Custodial Worker, Medical Support Assistance, Food
Service Worker, and Healthcare Engineer.
This report is a descriptive review and is intended for informational purposes. The OIG did not
make any recommendations.
VA OIG 26-01046-271 | Page 7 | September 11, 2026

Note: Occupations designated by at least 20 percent of facilities were included in this table. The OIG excludes the
Medical Officer and Nurse OPM occupational series level occupations in this analysis because VHA utilizes
assignment codes to designate specialties within the corresponding OPM occupational series. Assessing severe
shortages at the assignment code level instead of the broader occupational series codes enabled the OIG to assess
severe shortages for the Medical Officer and Nurse series in more detail. Naming conventions for occupations
provided in this table are as provided by VHA.
*Denotes assignment codes within the Medical Officer occupational series.
‡Denotes assignment codes within the Nurse occupational series.
§Wage Grade refers to the pay plan for the given Federal Wage System occupation, regulated under Title 5.

Appendix E: Office of the Under Secretary for Health
Memorandum
Department of Veterans Affairs Memorandum
Date: August 27, 2026
From: Office of the Under Secretary for Health (10)
Subj: National Review—OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s Severe Occupational
Staffing Shortages Fiscal Year 2026 (VIEWS 15102742).
To: Assistant Inspector General for Healthcare Inspections (54)
1. Thank you for the opportunity to review and comment on OIG’s draft report. The Veterans Health
Administration (VHA) notes that the OIG did not make any recommendations. VHA has no comments
and appreciates OIG’s comprehensive review.
2. Comments regarding the contents of this memorandum may be directed to the GAO OIG
Accountability Liaison Office.
(Original signed by:)
John Figueroa
